TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

* What Does Morocco’s First Woman Leader Actually Mean for the Country?

  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. On Sept. 29, King Mohammed VI named Fatima Ezzahra Mansouri as Morocco’s head of government, making the country the second in the Arab world after Tunisia to appoint a woman to lead its executive branch. Soon, images of Mansouri meeting with the king, both of them dressed in traditional outfits of the same light-blue shade, circulated around the world. It’s a moment that would have been unimaginable not so long ago. Mansouri’s appointment brought excitement to an otherwise uneventful election that was largely ignored by many Moroccans. The vote, which took place on Sept. 23, had an abstention rate of almost 62 percent, the country’s highest in almost 20 years. The low turnout was the culmination of decades-long apathy toward elections, with citizens feeling increasingly disconnected from Morocco’s political parties. On Sept. 29, King Mohammed VI named Fatima Ezzahra Mansouri as Morocco’s head of government, making the country the second in the Arab world after Tunisia to appoint a woman to lead its executive branch. Soon, images of Mansouri meeting with the king, both of them dressed in traditional outfits of the same light-blue shade, circulated around the world. It’s a moment that would have been unimaginable not so long ago. Mansouri’s appointment brought excitement to an otherwise uneventful election that was largely ignored by many Moroccans. The vote, which took place on Sept. 23, had an abstention rate of almost 62 percent, the country’s highest in almost 20 years. The low turnout was the culmination of decades-long apathy toward elections, with citizens feeling increasingly disconnected from Morocco’s political parties. Yet while Mansouri’s new role is symbolically important, she will have limited power, as executive authority rests with the king. Still, her leadership comes at a pivotal moment for the country, and she will have a chance to influence issues that are important to many citizens, including the much-anticipated reform of Morocco’s family status law. If she succeeds in shaping policies that improve Moroccans’ lives and support gender equality, she may very well leave her mark on a country that is striving to modernize even as it has long leaned conservative. Mansouri’s nomination has not been particularly surprising to observers of Moroccan politics. * Palestinian Voters Gave Eisenkot a Chance to Govern—He Should Take It
  Foreign Policy | 05 Nov 17:39 UTC
  Calling the Israeli election a deadlock is a misnomer.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Much of the analysis since Israel’s election on Oct. 27 has described the result as a deadlock—a stalemate between the bloc led by Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu and the opposition led by Gadi Eisenkot. According to this framing, neither side managed to clinch a majority in the 120-member parliament, with Netanyahu and his satellite parties mustering 53 seats and Eisenkot and his bloc capturing 48. As a result, Israel now finds itself at a political impasse. But this conclusion is based on faulty arithmetic. It fails to take into account the single most striking aspect of the election: the fact that Palestinian citizens of Israel, who make up roughly 20 percent of the population, voted in unprecedented numbers, helping two Palestinian-led parties capture a record 19 parliamentary seats. Their votes were an explicit repudiation of Netanyahu and his policies, which means that their representatives in parliament should be counted as part of the opposition bloc—bringing the tally to 67 seats. By any objective accounting, this is not a deadlock; it’s a win for the opposition, or at least, it should be framed as such. --- Energy (4) ---

* Analysis: China’s quarterly CO2 falls 3% in largest drop since Covid
  Carbon Brief | 06 Nov 00:01 UTC
  China’s carbon dioxide (CO2) emissions fell by 3.1% in the third quarter of 2026, as the Hormuz crisis continued to… The post Analysis: China’s quarterly CO2 falls 3% in largest drop since Covid appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: China’s carbon dioxide (CO2) emissions fell by 3.1% in the third quarter of 2026, as the Hormuz crisis continued to hit oil demand and coal use also dropped. Lauri Myllyvirta, lead analyst at the Centre for Research on Energy and Clean Air This was the largest quarterly reduction since the 2022 Covid lockdowns and means that China’s CO2 emissions have now fallen by an estimated 0.5% in the year to date. The new analysis for Carbon Brief, based on official figures and commercial data, shows that coal use fell by 3% year-on-year in the third quarter of 2026, while oil was down 4%. The main drivers were a fall in power-sector coal consumption, a continued sharp reduction in transport oil consumption and slowing growth in the coal-to-chemical industry. The sharp decline in China’s CO2 emissions extends a “ flat or falling ” trend that began in March 2024 and has now lasted more than two years, as shown in the figure below. The outlook for China’s CO2 emissions depends on the balance between rising energy demand, clean-energy growth and the government response to a GDP slowdown . Key drivers of the fall in CO2 in the third quarter of 2026 included a 3.3% drop in coal-power generation, after demand growth slowed and the amount of “ wasted ” wind and solar fell. Power generation from solar, wind and nuclear increased by 110 terawatt hours (TWh) year-on-year, more than twice as much as the growth in total electricity demand. This added clean-power generation equal to Germany’s total power output in the same time period. The fall in coal-power output and CO2 emissions came despite the fact that China continues to build new coal-power capacity at near-record rates . This means that average running hours for coal plants are falling even faster than total generation, eroding their profits. Meanwhile, transport oil consumption continued to fall sharply, down 9% year-on-year in the third quarter of 2026. Electric vehicles (EVs) continued to gain market share, hitting a record 67% of passenger car sales and 50% of heavy trucks in September. The amount of electricity used by EVs grew by 53%, much faster than the number of EVs on the road, showing increased usage of the existing fleet in preference to fuelled vehicles. Elsewhere, growth in the coal-to-chemical industry continued to slow down in the third quarter of 2026. The sector has been a key source of emissions growth, but high coal prices have weakened profitability and higher output prices hurt downstream demand . F
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/analysis-chinas-quarterly-co2-falls-3-in-largest-drop-since-covid

* Interactive: Tracking negotiating texts at the COP31 climate summit
  Carbon Brief | 05 Nov 14:13 UTC
  The centrepiece of every UN climate summit is for countries to negotiate the wording of a large number of legal… The post Interactive: Tracking negotiating texts at the COP31 climate summit appeared first on Carbon Brief .
  FULL TEXT: The centrepiece of every UN climate summit is for countries to negotiate the wording of a large number of legal agreements – and COP31 in the Turkish city of Antalya is no different. These texts are hashed out behind closed doors in the “ blue zone ” at the COP, where diplomats from nearly 200 nations – not including the US – will be haggling over each individual verb . Over the course of the two-week summit, negotiators will be trying to reach consensus on more than 100 separate agreements – but, first, they must agree which issues are on the agenda. The complexity of this process can make it challenging to keep track of what countries are fighting about and how negotiations are progressing. Carbon Brief’s real-time text tracker, below, offers a helping hand by decoding the agenda and keeping a searchable record of every document for each part of the negotiations. The first column of the interactive tracker lists the topic of each agenda item, with further columns including dates, page counts and links to the original PDFs. The table is searchable via the interactive text box and can be sorted using the arrow icons. This year’s talks come with a twist: Turkey holds the presidency and is hosting COP31, but Australia is jointly running the show as “ president of negotiations ”. The pair have already announced their intention to produce a “cover text” – an overarching political document summarising key negotiated outcomes, along with issues not on the official agenda. This could include a recognition that warming is set to “ overshoot ” the 1.5C limit and the impacts this is already causing . It may also include potential solutions, such as the Turkish presidency’s “ electrification ” target, for 35% of global final energy to come from electricity by 2035. While the summit is not seen as a banner year for the negotiations, falling half-way between two “ global stocktakes ” of progress, there are still plenty of major topics up for discussion. These include efforts to finalise the “ global goal on adaptation ” and whether to continue a contested process on how to cut emissions, known as the “ mitigation work programme ”. Negotiators will also be trying to agree on the details of a new “ just transition mechanism ”, also referred to as the “Belém-Antalya mechanism for global just transitions” (BAM). While most of the items on the agenda are agreed in advance, parties can suggest late additions. These must be signed off before negotiations can begin, often resu
  https://www.carbonbrief.org/interactive-tracking-negotiating-texts-at-the-cop31-climate-summit

Earlier this year, I wrote in New Lines about how a proposed hyperscale data center in Utah sparked a civic revolt over sweetheart deals, corruption and lack of trust between citizens and their leaders in a reliably Republican county. The resulting uproar saw the longtime Utah State Senate president ousted in a primary challenge, and on Wednesday, local Democrats broke a decades-long supermajority in the state by flipping more than a dozen seats in the legislature.   Longreads | 05 Nov 13:00 UTC
  "Feral parakeets flock to London."
  FULL TEXT: London’s feral parakeets are an invasive species—but they are also beautiful, and David Wingrave takes the chance to linger on their nightly roost at Hither Green Cemetery before asking whether they should be here at all. As calls for culls grow—backed by fears of crop damage, disease, and pressure on native wildlife—he probes what we really know about the harm these birds cause. Along the way, he asks deeper questions: how we decide where an animal “belongs,” when (if ever) it becomes native, and why we are so uneasy about seeing bright green birds in London’s parks. The fix O’Gorman endorses may sound drastic, but his precautionary principle is shared by invasion biology, the subdiscipline devoted to the study of species transplanted from their native ranges. There is a long history of organisms moved by humans, whether deliberately or inadvertently, wreaking total havoc upon their new homes. The brown tree snake, native to Indonesia, northern Australia, and Papua New Guinea, was accidentally introduced to Guam by the US military following World War II and promptly ate its way through ten of the island’s twelve endemic forest bird species. Similarly, Nile perch released into Lake Victoria by British colonial forces gulped down roughly two hundred species of homegrown cichlids, fish found nowhere else on Earth. Cane toads in Australia, Miconia trees on Hawaii and Tahiti, rats just about everywhere—the list goes on. And if biodiversity loss, however regrettable, seems like an abstraction in the scheme of things, know that human culture frequently suffers direct impoverishments because of invasives. Chestnut blight, discovered in the United States in 1904 after chestnut trees were imported from Japan, effectively deleted the American chestnut tree from the continent; its products were central to the diet and lifestyles of various indigenous peoples and communities across Appalachia. Phylloxera, an aphid relative, was imported to Europe on American grapevines during the second half of the nineteenth century. The subsequent infestation resulted in the destruction, by some estimates, of 90 percent of all European vineyards. “Godwits make the longest non-stop migration in the bird world. That might be the least of their marvels. Can they survive a changing planet?” “A couple of years after I began volunteering, I realized I was hell-bent on healing birds because they, too, had healed me—in more ways than one.” “Millions of songbirds in Southeast Asia are trappe
  https://longreads.com/2026/11/05/the-parakeets-london-invasive-species/

  FULL TEXT: (Photo by: Egyptian Presidency/Handout/Anadolu via Getty Images) Egypt is flexing its muscles as it prepares to help defend Saudi Arabia from Yemen’s Houthis. The decision reflects a commitment to protect the kingdom, but it could also drag Egypt into a regional conflict that continues to escalate. While Egypt clearly views Saudi security as intricately tied to regional stability, any military maneuver risks provoking a sustained retaliation. Strikes targeting Egyptian infrastructure could weaken its already fragile economy, resulting in even more domestic and regional unrest. That partly explains Egypt’s traditionally cautious foreign policy, which has continued under President Abdel Fattah el-Sisi. In 2015, Egypt did not partake in the Saudi-led coalition against the Houthis, nor did it join the recent Mecca Pact — a defense alliance between Turkey, Saudi Arabia and Pakistan. Egypt now believes that staying on the sidelines threatens its security more than asserting its might. The Houthis are already trying to change Egypt’s assessment. On Oct. 27, the Iran-backed group stated that they consider Cairo’s decision to aid Riyadh a “declaration of war.” The statement came a day after Egypt’s Parliament approved Sisi’s request to provide military support to Saudi Arabia if called upon to do so. Ahmed Aboudouh, an associate fellow of the Middle East and North Africa Program at Chatham House, believes that Egypt is preparing to proactively manage the risks. He told New Lines that the operations were likely to be defensive, but that this could mean not only defending from within Saudi territory, but also defending Egypt by attempting to limit the Houthis’ ability to attack Egyptian targets. “My sense is that Egypt’s role will be confined to air and naval defense,” Aboudouh added. He explained that Saudi Arabia’s vast size and critical assets make it difficult to intercept the barrage of Houthi airstrikes — a challenge that Egypt could help with. On Oct. 10, the Houthis hit Riyadh International Airport , killing 13 people and injuring more than 300. It was the deadliest attack on the kingdom in more than a decade. In addition, Aboudouh expects Egyptian naval vessels to enter the Bab al-Mandab Strait to protect the strategic route from Houthi attacks. Commercial ships traverse this critical strait to reach the Suez Canal, a lifeline for Egypt’s economy and one of its most reliable sources of foreign currency. * Why Immigration Agents Don’t Quit in Protest
  Longreads | 05 Nov 12:00 UTC
  "How a culture of dehumanization and denial keeps ICE and Border Patrol personnel 'at war with an enemy that does not fight back.'”
  FULL TEXT: Before he became an author and professor, Francisco Cantú was a border patrol agent. He’s written extensively about his experience, and here, he tries to understand why more of his one-time colleagues haven’t quit as the second Trump administration has ramped up its draconian immigration enforcement: Officers are acculturated to see migrants as entirely unlike them, despite the fact that more than half of Border Patrol’s twenty-one thousand agents and nearly a third of ICE’s twenty-two thousand agents are Latinos, many only a generation or two removed from their own family’s migration. They come to think of migrants and asylum seekers as potential gangsters, rapists, or petty criminals, whose asylum claims are almost always suspect, their stories exaggerated to take advantage of the freedoms and generosity of the state. It’s one thing to theorize about this dehumanization but something else to hear it plainly acknowledged by the people enacting it. So I remember I went home and I cried. You don’t want to treat humans like that.” “In Southern California’s Otay Mountain Wilderness, the federal surveillance network seems unavoidable. But year after year, migrant deaths persist.” “In the early 1980s, in Tucson, activists and religious leaders joined forces to protect refugees at the U.S.-Mexico border. Ashley Stimpson | Longreads | November 5, 2026 | 18 minutes (4,985 words) In the months leading up to the nation’s bicentennial, the rickety wooden wheels of covered wagons began to roll in every corner of the United States—from the evergreen forests of Washington and windswept prairies of North Dakota to the granite peaks of Maine and palm tree-lined suburbs of Central Florida. Over the next year, the Bicentennial Wagon Train (BWT) would commemorate the founding of the country by performing the reverse of Westward Expansion, as five caravans—eventually made up of as many as 300 horse-drawn wagons—snaked their way toward Valley Forge, where they would arrive on July 4, 1976, just in time to celebrate America’s 200th birthday. Along the way, the wagon trains would stop in hundreds of cities and towns, where residents could watch teams of wagoneers construct historically accurate encampments and perform a musical revue. If so moved, citizens could also sign a so-called rededication scroll, to recommit themselves to building a more perfect union. “To commemorate this nation’s Bicentennial, we hereby dedicate ourselves anew to the precepts of our Founding Fathers,” each signer promised, inking their name on a sheath of scratchy parchment paper. Large community dinners and solemn ceremonies were planned around these vows to democracy; in some cities, whole elementary schools and entire churches lined up to sign. To accommodate those not on the wagons’ path, scroll-signing centers were set up in Holiday Inns from coast to coast. The scrolls were then loaded onto the Mayflower moving van that accompanied each train and carried toward Pennsylvania, where organizers planned to have them microfilmed and placed in an aluminum time capsule interred in the Pennsylvania dirt for the next 100 years. In 2076, organizers of the Bicentennial Wagon Train imagined, future Americans would unearth these scrolls and feel a deep sense of appreciation and admiration for the Americans of the past. Altogether, 22 million people (a tenth of the population at the time) signed approximately 917,000 scrolls, a stack of paper as high as a 20-story building . * Believing and thinking
  Aeon | 03 Nov 11:00 UTC
  The separation of church and state has an unspoken psychological basis: we choose what we believe, but not what we think - by Neil Van Leeuwen & Jacob Eisler Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Fryeburg, Maine, US. Photo by Andrew Lichtenstein/Corbis/Getty Images The idea of separating church and state – now entrenched in many modern liberal democracies – is in some ways strange. After all, governments must keep themselves informed and inform their citizens. That’s why they turn to the science of meteorology to predict weather patterns, to economics to understand patterns of growth and decline, and to the medical sciences to improve public health. So why wouldn’t governments also keep close alliances with religious institutions to know what’s going on with God? Separating church and state appears strange from other perspectives as well. In the historical sweep of large-scale human societies, it’s a relative newcomer, often associated with the United States’ founding father Thomas Jefferson. In 1802, Jefferson asserted the principle of separation in a letter to the Danbury Baptist Association in Connecticut: We’re fans of Jefferson’s principle, one of us (Neil Van Leeuwen) being a philosophy professor who grew up in the Christian Reformed Church, and the other (Jacob Eisler) being a law professor who regularly attends synagogue. But Jefferson’s letter makes putting the principle into practice sound simpler than it is – as though governments could be entirely hands-off when it comes to people’s beliefs, or ‘opinions’. That, however, can’t be right: engaging with and shaping political, social and other kinds of beliefs is actually a core function of government. Public education guides what people believe about everything from mathematics to history to astronomy. Governments also shape beliefs for policy reasons. If fire department officials learn that people believe a building is safe when it isn’t, they will try to change what people believe about the building’s safety. Belief is also a category of legal interest. If a psychotherapist, for example, believes a patient is a danger to himself or others, the therapist is obligated by law to report that belief. All this means that Jefferson’s ‘wall of separation’ can’t sensibly prohibit governments from intervening on beliefs of any type whatsoever. Rather, Jefferson – along with advocates of the principle who came after him – must have presupposed that there is something distinctive about religious beliefs that makes it reasonable to give them a different legal status. But what is this distinctive thing? Is it that religious beliefs concern God? No, because Buddhism is a religion with its own beliefs, 
  https://aeon.co/essays/the-psychology-of-separating-church-and-state

* The self-making world
  Aeon | 02 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Long mocked and misunderstood, Lamarck’s radical idea that organisms shape themselves and their world is making a comeback - by Jessica Riskin Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: A pod of humpback whales making its way through Alaska’s Inside Passage. Courtesy of Far North Footage/Getty Images The French naturalist Jean-Baptiste Lamarck was 65 when his great treatise on the transformation of life was published. With Philosophie zoologique (1809), translated a century later as Zoological Philosophy , Lamarck intended to establish an entirely new science, one built on the idea that all living beings are constantly transforming themselves, from the tiniest microbes to the most complex mammals, gaining new forms and new abilities. It wasn’t the bodily structures of organisms that determined their ways of life, Lamarck said, but just the opposite: their ways of life in response to their circumstances gave rise to their changing bodily forms. ‘Can there be any consideration in natural history more important,’ he demanded, ‘or deserving of greater attention, than the one I have just set forth?’ (My translation.) But if you’ve heard anything at all about Lamarck, it’s probably how wrong he was about one animal in particular, the giraffe, and how this giraffe fiasco epitomises his wrongness about everything . Lamarck suggested that, by stretching to reach leaves in high tree branches, giraffes slightly elongated their necks, creating tiny changes that were then handed down to their offspring, accumulating over many generations to produce the long necks we see today. Around the turn of the 20th century, Lamarck’s giraffe idea became a laughing stock when biologists roundly rejected the notion that bodily changes undergone in the course of an individual lifetime could be inherited in the next generation. Illustration from Histoire naturelle, générale et particulière (1749-1804) by Georges-Louis Leclerc, Comte de Buffon. Courtesy the Linda Hall Library It wasn’t only his ideas about long-necked mammals that came under attack. Lamarck’s interest in giraffes was just a passing mention in the midst of his voluminous writings, which set out a revolutionary, field-founding theory: organisms, he claimed, transform themselves and, in doing so, they remake the world. That idea has drawn scorn for more than two centuries. Though Lamarck is often overlooked, his science was foundational to modern biology and informed the theory of evolution that Charles Darwin later developed. Darwin followed Lamarck in believing in the agency of living beings to transform themselves and their environment. But most people don’t know about Lamarck’s influence on Darwin b
  https://aeon.co/essays/the-self-making-world-of-jean-baptiste-lamarck

* Yemen’s War Has Turned Facebook Into a Mourning Wall

  As fighting intensified this fall, Yemenis turned Facebook into a running record of the dead. Hundreds of posts trace the lives of students and graduates who felt they had no choice but to take up arms. The post Yemen’s War Has Turned Facebook Into a Mourning Wall appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: Until early September, my Facebook feed looked much as it always had. Posts from friends, relatives and people with whom I shared mutual friends revolved around everyday life: weddings and engagement celebrations, condolences for elderly people who had died and the occasional tragedy of a traffic accident. Other posts reflected Yemenis’ daily struggles: prolonged power cuts, disruptions to education, a worsening economic crisis and the familiar long queues outside gas stations. Then, on Sept. 3, Houthi fighters launched a major military offensive against government-held areas of western Yemen, seizing six districts within a week. A month later, the internationally recognized Yemeni government responded with Operation Dawn of Yemen, aimed not only at recapturing lost territory but also at driving the Houthis out of other areas under their control. As fighting intensified across the front lines, an unusual number of mourning posts began appearing in my Facebook feed. Facebook remains Yemen’s public square, a place where people mourn their dead, celebrate milestones, document their lives and argue fiercely over the country’s politics. By early October, as the Houthis advanced deeper into government-held areas of Taiz province and approached the eponymous city itself, my feed had turned into an endless stream of obituaries. Parents grieved for their sons. Wives mourned their husbands. Friends wrote heartbreaking tributes to men they had known since childhood. As I began reading and collecting these posts, Facebook’s algorithm appeared to take notice of my interest. Each time I opened the platform, more photographs of the dead appeared, many shared by people who were not even on my friends list. Scrolling through Facebook began to feel like walking past a wall covered with photographs of the dead. “Hisham, the hero, my brother and beloved martyr, please come back, you troublemaker. My heart aches from your absence,” wrote Shehab al-Shehabi, a journalist, on Facebook on Oct. 13, sharing a photograph of Hisham, who looked barely out of childhood, dressed in a shirt, with a tan tactical vest. Unlike in Houthi-controlled areas, where information about fighters’ deaths appears to be tightly controlled and casualties are often announced through official funerals, families and friends in government-controlled areas openly share photographs, names and personal stories of those killed on the battlefield, making public expressions of grief more visible and spontaneous.   https://newlinesmag.com/spotlight/yemens-war-has-turned-facebook-into-a-mourning-wall/

  FULL TEXT: On Oct. 8, U.S. Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth strode across the deck of the USS Abraham Lincoln to deliver one of his trademark blasts of wrath. In an interview with the MAGA influencer Jack Posobiec for the right-wing Real America’s Voice outlet, Hegseth ripped into the media for its critical coverage of the ship’s condition and the war that had caused its marathon service. But Hegseth’s most animated comments came when he was asked about the upcoming execution of Nidal Hasan, who murdered 12 soldiers and one civilian at Fort Hood on Nov. 5, 2009. Announcing that Hasan would face a firing squad on Dec. 3, Hegseth remarked, “We’ll make sure that people are able to watch it. That it’s public.” With the internet teeming with videos of extrajudicial aerial strikes against alleged drug runners and point-of-view clips of drones obliterating targets in Iran, the moment marked another addition to Hegseth’s carnival of death. The response to Hegseth’s plan was bipartisan disgust. Republican Rep. Austin Scott recoiled, posting on X that “live-streaming executions is what jihadists do,” while Democratic Sen. Jack Reed argued the “barbaric spectacle” would serve as “a gift to anti-American terrorist recruiters.” Even while battling cancer, Pope Leo XIV weighed in, writing on X that the death penalty is “inadmissible” because “the common good can be safeguarded and the requirement of justice can be met without recourse to capital punishment.” The pontiff concluded his post with the hashtag #NoDeathPenalty. Recognizing that he was playing a losing hand, on Oct. 15 President Donald Trump announced on Truth Social that “this event will not be streamed.” Trump’s cancellation statement was as unhinged as Hegseth’s original declaration: blaming the tragedy on an alleged “intelligence failure” by the Obama administration, linking Hasan’s killing spree with the actions of “Terrorists and SCUM” and invoking the discredited claim that the death penalty serves as a deterrent. The firing squad may not be livestreamed, but the White House’s carnival of death — its illegal aerial strikes in the Caribbean and Pacific, its posting footage of kill shots in Iran, its murderous ICE campaign and more — is in full swing. * In praise of the dilettante
  Aeon | 30 Oct 10:00 UTC [also carried by: Longreads]
  A wayfaring cosmopolitan who never gets stuck, the dilettante is the right anti-hero for our hyper-specialised age - by Jay Miller Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Jean Cocteau, poet, artist, filmmaker, photographed in 1948 by Philippe Halsman. Courtesy Magnum Photos Why, of all people, defend the dilettante ? An amateur at best, a flighty rascal at worst: why take an interest in someone whose own interests are never more than a passing hobby? These are serious times – why take seriously someone for whom nothing is serious? Merriam-Webster defines the dilettante as ‘a person having a superficial interest in an art or a branch of knowledge’. Encyclopedia.com calls the dilettante someone who cultivates an interest ‘without real commitment or knowledge’. Cambridge settles for someone whose understanding ‘is not very deep or serious’. Collins gives us someone who ‘seems interested in a subject … but does not really know very much about it’. None of it is flattering. Clearly, the dilettante is no aspirational character. No university markets itself as training the next generation of dilettantes; no magazine runs a ‘dilettante of the year’ issue (except, perhaps, for the excellent Idler publication). He – for the dilettante is, historically and in popular imagination, so often a ‘he’ – is in essence an amateur, a role defined by wide interests and narrow expertise. Such is the reputation of the dilettante, assigned and maintained by a culture that treats a single-minded devotion to one pursuit as the only route to a meaningful life. Pick something and stick with it, we are told. It’s not so much an explicit campaign against dilettantism I’m challenging, but rather the diffuse yet relentless pressure to narrow one’s interests, to specialise, to gain expertise, to forgo breadth for the sake of depth. We hear it in the barrage of imperatives to follow your dreams or find your passion uttered at graduation speeches, in self-help guides, and similarly corporate-sounding pep talks. Under this scheme, the specialist is the hero and the dilettante is but a cautionary tale: the person who never committed, never finished, never got good enough at any one thing. But what if the scheme has it backwards? What if the dilettante’s perpetual movement from one thing to another is not a failure of nerve but a different and defensible way of being, one whose exploratory zeal turns out to be just what a hyper-specialised, over-optimised and all-too- serious world needs most? Then the dilettante would turn out to be not a capricious buffoon but a kind of modern anti-hero. That, I argue, is a dilettantism worth defending. F irst, let’s consider
  https://aeon.co/essays/why-the-dilettante-is-the-anti-hero-of-our-over-optimised-age
